# Excalidraw Data

## Text Elements
Key Concepts & Definitions Chapter 1-6 ^ksGkjRxs

Categorical: Data that represents groups or categories (e.g., eye color, gender, zip codes). ^dsMgWOQN

Quantitative: Numerical data that represents amounts or measurements (e.g., height, weight, test scores). ^JkcoRywX

Example: A dataset with students' favorite colors (categorical) and their heights (quantitative). ^RMYg0Fzh

Descriptive Statistics ^yEJnRz1E

Mean: Average value of a dataset. ^0MNlHZnX

Median: Middle value when data is ordered. ^OLxH4FMH

Mode: Most frequently occurring value. ^7ThL0BI9

Variance & Standard Deviation: Measure how spread out the data is. ^bmOmiXAt

Example: Given test scores [70, 80, 85, 90, 95], the mean is 84, median is 85, and standard deviation shows how much scores deviate from the mean. ^sfbBhb9W

Distribution Shapes ^hKCfCrR6

Symmetric: Left and right sides mirror each other. ^xLvkt2tS

Skewed Left: Tail on the left (e.g., exam scores where most students score high but a few score very low). ^YdnDo421

Skewed Right: Tail on the right (e.g., income distribution where most people earn lower amounts but a few earn very high). ^eCy8LZEb

Example: A histogram showing salaries where most employees earn $50,000 but a few executives earn $500,000 (right-skewed). ^mgg1ugXJ

Boxplots & Five-Number Summary ^VbQgRX0B

Five-Number Summary: 
 ^H3EXW6yl

Boxplot: A visual representation of the five-number summary. ^4R6r4odz

Normal Distribution ^I0i0dMPi

Bell-shaped, symmetric curve where most values cluster around the mean. ^CqUusZmr

68-95-99.7 Rule: 68% of data falls within 1 standard deviation, 95% within 2, and 99.7% within 3. ^vUGMsRB0

Z-score ^VO63IfK9

Measures how many standard deviations a value is from the mean. ^h8aBBe87

Formula:  ^YZPUbnF1

Example: If SAT scores have a mean of 500 and standard deviation of 100, a score of 650 has a z-score of (650-500)/100 = 1.5. ^eJrANH2q

Correlation (r) ^scBrbTEM

Measures strength & direction of a linear relationship between two variables (-1 to 1). ^HMDEMa1k

Example: Height and weight have a strong positive correlation (~0.8), meaning taller people tend to weigh more. ^rFP6Uule

Regression Equation ^CZvY74ci

Describes the relationship between dependent (Y) and independent (X) variables. ^I1hmrist

Formula:  ^pLCo96W5

Example: Predicting weight (Y) based on height (X), where y = 50 + 0.5x means for every 1 cm increase in height, weight increases by 0.5 kg. ^C9GVGWUS

Residuals ^k1XfHFGE

Difference between actual and predicted values in regression. ^KXULVHl7

Formula:  ^gaMplPgm

Example: If actual weight is 80 kg and predicted is 78 kg, residual is 80 - 78 = 2. ^rYN5uE7u

Relative Risk (RR) ^AI7iswWT

Compares risk between two groups. ^JZlUSolg

Formula: RR = P(disease | exposed) / P(disease | unexposed) ^joYP0Vwk

Example: Suppose we are studying lung cancer risk in smokers and non-smokers. The data is: ^HZcFm5Gg

Data Types ^FvOj83sj

Now, calculate the Relative Risk: RR = (0.10/0.02) = 5 This means that smokers are 5 times more likely to develop lung cancer compared to non-smokers. ^HUCiczHR

RR=1: No difference in risk between the two groups. ^8V4s6Sph

RR>1: Higher risk in the exposed group (risk factor). ^irNnL1tz

RR<1: Lower risk in the exposed group (protective factor). ^XwwFzQr1

Odds Ratio (OR) ^XXowYdkj

Compares odds of an event occurring in two groups. ^9k58U0es

Formula: OR = (p1 / (1 - p1)) / (p0 / (1 - p0)) ^xSADuH8j

Where: ^FdMaP5wP

p1​ = Probability of the event in the exposed group. ^dwNXokct

p0​ = Probability of the event in the unexposed group. ^OFhueO3q

1−p1​ and 1−p0​ are the probabilities of the event not occurring in the respective groups. ^0wAgrBTP

Example: If 20% of exposed people develop a disease while 5% of non-exposed do, OR = (0.2/0.8) / (0.05/0.95) = 4. ^iGZUCZ46

Standard Normal Table ^8igpzpgJ

Used to find probabilities for standard normal (Z) scores. ^LyVHHKyK

Example: If Z = 1.5, the table shows P(Z < 1.5) ≈ 0.933. ^k1dUJF5r

Important Formulas & Explanations ^MnpAn51W

Mean (x̄) ^JNrGGyvK

Formula: ^71c7ZNvc

Explanation: The sum of all observations divided by the number of observations. ^OOufkT51

Example: For the numbers [5, 10, 15], mean = (5+10+15)/3 = 10. ^xu7bC6o6

Variance (s²) ^dOhJ9zYq

Formula:  ^wENmtirG

Explanation: Measures the spread of the data from the mean. ^tYLXvZe9

Example: If x̄ = 10 and data points are [5, 10, 15], variance = ((5-10)² + (10-10)² + (15-10)²) / (3-1) = 25. ^arajNnww

Standard Deviation (s) ^8bBIG8Sg

Formula:  ^5ep5jqhj

Explanation: The square root of variance gives the average deviation from the mean. ^S4PSQpRy

Correlation (r) ^e3GCikeh

Formula:  ^Va83FnSW

Explanation: Measures the strength and direction of a linear relationship between two variables. ^sgltmFaR

Example: If taller people tend to weigh more, r is positive. ^IFEQDNiX

Regression Slope (b) ^SW5kbm3H

Formula:  ^Am6DW6zU

Explanation: Represents the change in y for a one-unit increase in x. ^xA17cB9q

Example: If height increases by 1 cm, weight increases by 0.5 kg, b = 0.5. ^3k4VQhgk

Intercept (a) ^dYi9MQht

Formula:  ^9DD0TUP1

Explanation: The predicted value of y when x = 0. ^Nb0Hfjlc

Example: If the regression equation is weight = 50 + 0.5(height), then when height = 0, predicted weight = 50. ^5Pxxb38f

Key Concepts & Definitions Chapter 8–9, 12–13, 15–16, 32 ^EFo5vM10

Chapter 8: Producing Data – Sampling ^oGRXWQVb

Population: Entire group we want information about. ^ROWGQBRl

Sample: Subset of population actually studied. ^7V4EsE91

Census: Survey of the entire population. ^UFpbexz0

Sampling Design: How the sample is chosen. ^hkl5aMyX

Bias: Systematic error in design leading to misleading results. ^0WH333ZT

Random Sampling: Uses chance to select subjects. ^z9H2oSv6

Types of Sampling: ^h1gLOXTu

Simple Random Sample (SRS): Equal chance for all individuals/groups. ^0S4UcySG

Stratified Random Sample: Split population into strata (groups), sample each. ^7wVvs2Fg

Cluster Sample: Divide into clusters, randomly choose clusters. ^YIJXfctn

Multistage Sample: Combines multiple sampling methods. ^mBMqT4on

Sources of Bias: ^zONDtLRA

Undercoverage: Some groups left out. ^jn8RFAoJ

Nonresponse: Selected individuals don’t respond. ^4XpRqTd4

Response Bias: Inaccurate or dishonest answers. ^3bt9gvw8

Question Wording: Leading or unclear questions. ^mzRazFja

Chapter 9: Producing Data – Experiments ^z8NjvxQX

Observational Study: Observes individuals without influence. ^wDE6a7Te

Experiment: Applies treatment to study cause-and-effect. ^KQouNS1f

Treatment: Condition applied in an experiment. ^Sc0hSvgu

Experimental Units: Subjects of the experiment. ^qEpAjeCA

Factor: Explanatory variable. ^lPxgKc01

Level: Specific value of a factor. ^G9l5DURU

Response Variable: Outcome measured. ^VxEErNdr

Principles of Experimental Design: ^VUqZNY9x

Control: Reduce variability. ^ueBInxYx

Randomization: Use chance. ^DdI5VmZT

Replication: Use enough subjects to detect effects. ^0b0lnTLi

Designs: ^892WKLyq

Completely Randomized: Random assignment to groups. ^xZffuHlb

Randomized Comparative: Compare treatments. ^LJlCtczq

Matched Pairs: Each subject gets both treatments. ^NLeAiFam

Block Design: Subjects grouped and randomized within blocks. ^eM3FqYta

Key Terms: ^HUk8Mblv

Placebo: Dummy treatment. ^QxcUSti7

Placebo Effect: Psychological impact of receiving treatment. ^FGjhZlH5

Blinding: Subject/experimenter unaware of treatment. ^Q1adZcwU

Double-Blind: Both unaware. ^1WnKzZ52

Confounding: When variables' effects can't be separated. ^yMtHhZ0P

Chapter 12: Introducing Probability ^4hfi6Aa7

Probability: Likelihood of an event. ^BtI7wRLg

Experiment: Process with outcomes. ^vKNDSz9j

Sample Space (S): All possible outcomes. ^2SxW6Q7C

Event: Subset of outcomes. ^zURlsbZS

Random Phenomenon: Uncertain outcome, predictable pattern. ^qpDFtYHZ

Law of Large Numbers: With more trials, observed frequency approaches probability. ^oWjhHDw0

Rules of Probability: ^biojCfrQ

Discrete vs Continuous: ^INm2fqQ3

Discrete: Countable values (e.g., # of emails). ^mit6fSmE

Continuous: Measurable range (e.g., time, height). ^lmNq4t4l

Formula: 0≤P≤1 ^81MuMeVS

Explanation: Probabilities must be between 0 (impossible event) and 1 (certain event). ^o8w4TRba

Example: P(rolling a 7 on a standard die) = 0, P(rolling a number between 1–6) = 1. ^zA8kIzES

Formula: P(Sample Space)=1 ^YML6lUze

Explanation: The total probability of all possible outcomes in the sample space is always 1. ^pe4XGH5M

Example: For a six-sided die: P(1) + P(2) + ... + P(6) = 1. ^jC3Eia7i

Formula: P(A^c)=1−P(A) ^1CNgRZRR

Explanation: The probability that an event does not occur is one minus the probability that it does. ^5b4ZuZuC

Example: If P(rain) = 0.3, then P(no rain) = 1 - 0.3 = 0.7. ^b522MAYb

Formula: (if A and B are disjoint) ^cLXKow61

Explanation: If two events can't happen at the same time, their probabilities simply add. ^DzUVqsqr

Example: Drawing a king or queen from a deck: P(K) + P(Q) = 4/52 + 4/52 = 8/52. ^5N3NcPHg

Formula: P(A∪B)=P(A)+P(B)−P(A∩B) ^gbDwDtVt

Explanation: When two events can both occur, subtract the overlap to avoid double-counting. ^pBZ6CZ3B

Example: P(heart or king in a deck) = 13/52 + 4/52 - 1/52 = 16/52. ^rA7bTyGl

Formula: (if A and B are independent) ^bNRehYSu

Explanation: For independent events, multiply the probabilities to get the chance they both happen. ^2GlPM0ii

Example: P(head on coin and 6 on die) = 0.5 × 1/6 = 1/12. ^saQfkRjZ

Explanation: Conditional probability is the chance of A happening given that B has already occurred. ^T2Xn73UL

Example: If 70% of students are vaccinated, and 50% of vaccinated students take stats, then P(stats | vaccinated) = 0.5 ^jwRTW5el

Probability Range Rule ^o6ZQpFqP

Sample Space Rule (also called Total Probability Rule) ^HKNps2h0

Complement Rule ^LfX5H2Qj

Addition Rule for Disjoint Events ^ArO127FX

General Addition Rule ^V4ZXlyIJ

Multiplication Rule for Independent Events ^f7a7Dp5W

Conditional Probability Rule ^10Ojopel

Formula: P(A∣B)=P(A∩B)/P(B))​ ^xBG8K8FP
